========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I guess I'm here because I just can't shake this feeling that I'm... I'm a complete failure. I mean, I'm 18, I'm just starting out as a registered nurse, taking some college classes to get ahead, and I was so excited, you know? I thought this was my calling. But lately, I just feel like I'm an absolute danger to everyone around me. There was this... this near-miss at work recently. Nobody got hurt, thank God, but it was so close. And ever since then, it's like a loop playing in my head, over and over, showing me exactly how incompetent I am. It's like that one mistake just proves I'm not meant to be a nurse at all. I keep thinking about how much worse it could have been, and how I'm just a huge disappointment to my family, my instructors, the patients... everyone. Honestly, I've been having these thoughts that they'd all just be so much better off without me getting in the way and messing everything up. It's just this heavy, awful guilt that I can't turn off, no matter how hard I try to focus on my classes or my shifts.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Harming myself? I mean... no. No, I don't think so. Like, I don't have a specific plan to actually do anything to myself, if that's what you mean. I'm not sitting in my room figuring out a way to... you know, actually cause myself physical harm. I know as a nurse what that looks like and I don't have a plan like that. 

But it's just... it's more complicated than just a simple no, I guess. Because I keep having these thoughts, and they just sort of creep in, especially when I'm driving home from a shift or when I'm lying in bed and the whole house is quiet and my brain just won't shut off. I just start thinking about how much easier it would be if I just didn't wake up. Like, if I could just go to sleep and it would all just stop. The pressure, the guilt about that near-miss at work, the constant, sickening fear that I'm going to actually hurt a patient next time... it would all just be gone. 

I just keep imagining what it would be like if I could just completely disappear. Not violently, just... cease to exist? Because if I wasn't here, if I was just gone, then I couldn't mess anything up ever again. I wouldn't be this huge liability on the floor, and I wouldn't be letting my instructors down in my classes. It's just this really heavy, constant feeling that my being here is the actual problem. So I don't want to hurt myself, I really don't have any plan to do that, but I just... I constantly wish I could just fade away so I wouldn't be a risk to anyone anymore.

Well, it hasn't just been since that near-miss at work, if that's what you're thinking. I mean, that definitely made it all so much worse, like a million times worse, because suddenly I had proof, you know? Actual proof that I'm terrible at this. But the feeling itself... the feeling that I'm just not good enough and that I'm messing everything up... it's been going on for a lot longer than that. 

I've been trying to pinpoint exactly when it started getting this bad, and I really think it's been about... maybe a year and two months? Yeah, about that long. It really started right after I graduated high school. Because, you know, high school was one thing, but then suddenly I was thrown into all these college classes, trying to get my prerequisites done, and at the same time I started working full-time at the clinic. It was just this massive transition. I thought I was ready for it. I really did. I had this whole plan, and I was so eager to just dive in and start helping people. 

But almost immediately, it just felt like I was drowning. The classes were so much harder than I expected, and the clinic... well, the clinic is just so fast-paced and there's so much responsibility. Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. I'd come home exhausted, try to study, and just sit there staring at my textbooks, feeling like an absolute fraud. So yeah, it's been over a year of just carrying this around every single day, this constant weight telling me I'm a failure, long before that incident even happened. It just... it just keeps building up, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Joy? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I used to have things that I liked doing, things that actually helped me unwind. Like, back when I first started my classes, before everything got so heavy, I used to go jogging. There's this trail near my apartment, and I'd put on a podcast and just run for like three miles. It was a great way to clear my head, you know? Just me and the pavement. And if the weather was bad, I'd stay in and knit. I actually started knitting this huge, chunky blanket for my mom's birthday. I got the really expensive yarn in her favorite color, this deep plum purple. I was so excited about it. I'd sit there for hours, just doing the stitches, and it was so relaxing. 

I also used to love cooking. I'd find these complex recipes online and spend my whole Sunday afternoon prepping meals for the week. I made this amazing roasted red pepper pasta once... and I used to read a lot, too, mostly thrillers or historical fiction, just to escape for a bit after staring at nursing textbooks all day. 

But now... I don't know. It's like a switch flipped. I haven't been on that trail in months. The knitting is just sitting in a bag in the corner of my room, gathering dust. I haven't even looked at a recipe; I mostly just eat whatever is easiest, like a bowl of cereal or something, if I eat at all. And my books are just stacked on my nightstand untouched. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. I'll sit there for what feels like hours, just staring at the wall, still in my scrubs, and I just can't bring myself to care about any of the things I used to love. It all just feels so pointless now. Like, what's the point of knitting a blanket or going for a run when I'm such a mess at work? I just don't have the capacity for it anymore. It's just gone.

Honestly, I've been having these thoughts that they'd all just be so much better off without me getting in the way and messing everything up. It's just this heavy, awful guilt that I can't turn off, no matter how hard I try to focus on my classes or my shifts.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? I mean... no. No, I don't think so. Like, I don't have a specific plan to actually do anything to myself, if that's what you mean. I'm not sitting in my room figuring out a way to... you know, actually cause myself physical harm. I know as a nurse what that looks like and I don't have a plan like that. \n\nBut it's just... it's more complicated than just a simple no, I guess. Because I keep having these thoughts, and they just sort of creep in, especially when I'm driving home from a shift or when I'm lying in bed and the whole house is quiet and my brain just won't shut off. I just start thinking about how much easier it would be if I just didn't wake up. Like, if I could just go to sleep and it would all just stop. The pressure, the guilt about that near-miss at work, the constant, sickening fear that I'm going to actually hurt a patient next time... it would all just be gone. \n\nI just keep imagining what it would be like if I could just completely disappear. Not violently, just... cease to exist? Because if I wasn't here, if I was just gone, then I couldn't mess anything up ever again. I wouldn't be this huge liability on the floor, and I wouldn't be letting my instructors down in my classes. It's just this really heavy, constant feeling that my being here is the actual problem. So I don't want to hurt myself, I really don't have any plan to do that, but I just... I constantly wish I could just fade away so I wouldn't be a risk to anyone anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this overwhelming guilt and like you're a failure?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't just been since that near-miss at work, if that's what you're thinking. I mean, that definitely made it all so much worse, like a million times worse, because suddenly I had proof, you know? Actual proof that I'm terrible at this. But the feeling itself... the feeling that I'm just not good enough and that I'm messing everything up... it's been going on for a lot longer than that. \n\nI've been trying to pinpoint exactly when it started getting this bad, and I really think it's been about... maybe a year and two months? Yeah, about that long. It really started right after I graduated high school. Because, you know, high school was one thing, but then suddenly I was thrown into all these college classes, trying to get my prerequisites done, and at the same time I started working full-time at the clinic. It was just this massive transition. I thought I was ready for it. I really did. I had this whole plan, and I was so eager to just dive in and start helping people. \n\nBut almost immediately, it just felt like I was drowning. The classes were so much harder than I expected, and the clinic... well, the clinic is just so fast-paced and there's so much responsibility. Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. I'd come home exhausted, try to study, and just sit there staring at my textbooks, feeling like an absolute fraud. So yeah, it's been over a year of just carrying this around every single day, this constant weight telling me I'm a failure, long before that incident even happened. It just... it just keeps building up, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find joy or interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Joy? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I used to have things that I liked doing, things that actually helped me unwind. Like, back when I first started my classes, before everything got so heavy, I used to go jogging. There's this trail near my apartment, and I'd put on a podcast and just run for like three miles. It was a great way to clear my head, you know? Just me and the pavement. And if the weather was bad, I'd stay in and knit. I actually started knitting this huge, chunky blanket for my mom's birthday. I got the really expensive yarn in her favorite color, this deep plum purple. I was so excited about it. I'd sit there for hours, just doing the stitches, and it was so relaxing. \n\nI also used to love cooking. I'd find these complex recipes online and spend my whole Sunday afternoon prepping meals for the week. I made this amazing roasted red pepper pasta once... and I used to read a lot, too, mostly thrillers or historical fiction, just to escape for a bit after staring at nursing textbooks all day. \n\nBut now... I don't know. It's like a switch flipped. I haven't been on that trail in months. The knitting is just sitting in a bag in the corner of my room, gathering dust. I haven't even looked at a recipe; I mostly just eat whatever is easiest, like a bowl of cereal or something, if I eat at all. And my books are just stacked on my nightstand untouched. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. I'll sit there for what feels like hours, just staring at the wall, still in my scrubs, and I just can't bring myself to care about any of the things I used to love. Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. I'd come home exhausted, try to study, and just sit there staring at my textbooks, feeling like an absolute fraud. So yeah, it's been over a year of just carrying this around every single day, this constant weight telling me I'm a failure, long before that incident even happened. It just... it just keeps building up, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find joy or interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Joy? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I used to have things that I liked doing, things that actually helped me unwind. Like, back when I first started my classes, before everything got so heavy, I used to go jogging. There's this trail near my apartment, and I'd put on a podcast and just run for like three miles. It was a great way to clear my head, you know? Just me and the pavement. And if the weather was bad, I'd stay in and knit. I actually started knitting this huge, chunky blanket for my mom's birthday. I got the really expensive yarn in her favorite color, this deep plum purple. I was so excited about it. I'd sit there for hours, just doing the stitches, and it was so relaxing. \n\nI also used to love cooking. I don't know. It's like a switch flipped. I haven't been on that trail in months. The knitting is just sitting in a bag in the corner of my room, gathering dust. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. I'll sit there for what feels like hours, just staring at the wall, still in my scrubs, and I just can't bring myself to care about any of the things I used to love. It all just feels so pointless now. Like, what's the point of knitting a blanket or going for a run when I'm such a mess at work? I just don't have the capacity for it anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, it's definitely been exactly like that specifically over the last two weeks, too. I can't even force myself to care about finishing that blanket for my mom, even though her birthday is coming up. It's just this heavy, draining feeling where even the thought of opening a book or boiling water for pasta feels completely impossible. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I haven't really been on a scale or anything, so I can't give you an exact number, but... yeah, I think I have. I definitely have. It's not something I've been trying to do, you know? Like, I'm not on a diet or trying to count calories or anything like that. But I've noticed it mostly with my clothes. Like my work pants, the scrubs I wear to the clinic—they've been getting really loose lately. I actually had to safety-pin the waistband on my favorite pair the other day just so they wouldn't slide down while I was walking down the hall, which is just... it's embarrassing, honestly. 

But it makes sense, I guess, because my appetite is just completely gone. Like I was saying about the cooking, I just don't care about food anymore. I'll wake up, go to class or go to work, and I'll just completely forget to eat. The whole day will go by, and it won't even cross my mind. And then it'll be, like, four or five in the afternoon, and I'll realize I haven't had anything but water. Even then, I don't feel hungry. My stomach might rumble a little, but the thought of actually preparing a meal or even chewing something substantial just feels exhausting. So I might force down a handful of saltine crackers just to put something in my stomach, or I'll just drink another cup of coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, but it's warm and it's easy. So yeah, I'm basically just living on coffee and crackers, and it's definitely showing. My mom even made a comment about my face looking thinner when I FaceTimed her last week, which I tried to brush off, but... yeah, the weight is definitely dropping off, even though I'm not trying to lose it.

Elevated mood? Like... feeling really happy or having a lot of energy? No. No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt anything close to that. It's been the exact opposite for so long now. I mean, the past year has just been a constant, heavy drag. I don't wake up feeling refreshed or ready to take on the day, let alone feeling like I could conquer the world or something. No days where I suddenly felt great and wanted to do a million things. I haven't picked up extra shifts because I felt super motivated, or decided to deep clean my apartment, or suddenly wanted to go out with friends. It's just been this consistent, exhausting low. So, no, nothing like that at all. Not even for a day.

No... no, I haven't. I mean, I don't have a plan. I'm not sitting here figuring out how I would actually do it or, you know, looking things up or anything like that. I wouldn't actually hurt myself. I wouldn't have to drag myself out of bed or force down crackers or pretend I'm okay when I'm checking a patient's vitals. It would just all stop. The pressure would stop. But the feeling itself... the feeling that I'm just not good enough and that I'm messing everything up... it's been going on for a lot longer than that. \n\nI've been trying to pinpoint exactly when it started getting this bad, and I really think it's been about... maybe a year and two months? Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. Between nursing school and clinicals, it's just been a steady decline into this... this numbness. \n\nI definitely haven't had any bursts of energy. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's been a while now. I'd say about a year and two months, give or take. I remember it pretty clearly because it all started right after I graduated high school. You know, you have this idea in your head about what life is going to be like after graduation. Everyone talks about how it's this big, exciting new chapter, and you're supposed to feel so free and ready to take on the world. But for me, it was just... it was like hitting a wall. 

Right after graduation, I started taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic as a registered nurse, which I know is a lot for an 18-year-old to take on all at once. I thought I could handle it. I thought I was being responsible and getting a head start on my career. But almost immediately, things just started feeling wrong. The classes were harder than I expected, and the clinic was just non-stop. I remember driving home from my first week of full-time shifts and just crying in my car for twenty minutes before I could even go inside my apartment. And I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period, you know? Like, "Oh, you're just tired, you just need to get used to the schedule, everyone feels overwhelmed at first." 

But it didn't get better. The weeks turned into months, and instead of adjusting, I just started shutting down. The guilt started creeping in because I felt like I wasn't doing enough at work, or I wasn't studying enough for my classes, or I wasn't being a good enough daughter to my mom. And then the things I used to enjoy, like the cooking I mentioned, or even just watching a movie or talking to my friends—it all just started to feel like a chore. Like it took too much energy. And the appetite thing... that kind of snuck up on me, but looking back, I can trace it all back to that same time period, right after high school when everything just piled on. So yeah, it's been about fourteen months of just... this. Just trying to keep my head above water and feeling like I'm constantly drowning.

Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. I'd come home exhausted, try to study, and just sit there staring at my textbooks, feeling like an absolute fraud. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. It all just feels so pointless now. Like, what's the point of knitting a blanket or going for a run when I'm such a mess at work? I just don't have the capacity for it anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, it's definitely been exactly like that specifically over the last two weeks, too. Probably even worse recently, honestly. I'll look over at that knitting bag in the corner, or I'll see the books on my nightstand that I used to use to clear my head, and I just... I can't make myself care. I can't even force myself to care about finishing that blanket for my mom, even though her birthday is coming up. Just me, in my scrubs, sitting on the edge of the bed for hours doing absolutely nothing and just not being able to care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really been on a scale or anything, so I can't give you an exact number, but... yeah, I think I have. Hearing voices? Like... hallucinations? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I know I've been struggling a lot, and I feel completely overwhelmed and exhausted all the time, but I haven't lost touch with reality or anything like that. I'm not seeing things that aren't there or hearing people talking to me when I'm alone. 

It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because when I first started my clinicals at the clinic, we had a patient come in who was actively hallucinating, and it was so intense to witness. I remember thinking how terrifying it must be to not be able to trust your own senses. But for me, it's nothing like that. The only voice I hear is my own internal monologue, and honestly, that's bad enough right now because it's just constantly telling me how I'm failing at everything, how I'm a burden, how I can't keep up with my classes or my shifts. It's just this relentless, negative loop in my own head. But it's definitely my own thoughts, you know? Not some external voice or anything strange. 

And as for seeing things... no. The world looks exactly the same as it always has, it just feels a lot grayer and heavier. I'm just so tired. But my senses are all fine. I don't have any bizarre thoughts or beliefs, either. I know exactly who I am, where I am, and what's going on around me. I'm just... I'm just an 18-year-old girl who took on way too much, trying to balance nursing school and working as an RN, and I've just completely crashed. But no, no hallucinations or delusions or anything like that. Just the depression and the guilt and the exhaustion.

I wouldn't be this huge liability on the floor, and I wouldn't be letting my instructors down in my classes. It's just this really heavy, constant feeling that my being here is the actual problem. So I don't want to hurt myself, I really don't have any plan to do that, but I just... I constantly wish I could just fade away so I wouldn't be a risk to anyone anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this overwhelming guilt and like you're a failure?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't just been since that near-miss at work, if that's what you're thinking. I mean, that definitely made it all so much worse, like a million times worse, because suddenly I had proof, you know? Actual proof that I'm terrible at this. But the feeling itself... the feeling that I'm just not good enough and that I'm messing everything up... it's been going on for a lot longer than that. \n\nI've been trying to pinpoint exactly when it started getting this bad, and I really think it's been about... maybe a year and two months? Yeah, about that long. It really started right after I graduated high school. Because, you know, high school was one thing, but then suddenly I was thrown into all these college classes, trying to get my prerequisites done, and at the same time I started working full-time at the clinic. It was just this massive transition. I thought I was ready for it. I really did. I had this whole plan, and I was so eager to just dive in and start helping people. \n\nBut almost immediately, it just felt like I was drowning. The classes were so much harder than I expected, and the clinic... well, the clinic is just so fast-paced and there's so much responsibility. Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. I'd come home exhausted, try to study, and just sit there staring at my textbooks, feeling like an absolute fraud. So yeah, it's been over a year of just carrying this around every single day, this constant weight telling me I'm a failure, long before that incident even happened. It just... it just keeps building up, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find joy or interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Joy? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I used to have things that I liked doing, things that actually helped me unwind. Like, back when I first started my classes, before everything got so heavy, I used to go jogging. There's this trail near my apartment, and I'd put on a podcast and just run for like three miles. It was a great way to clear my head, you know? Just me and the pavement. And if the weather was bad, I'd stay in and knit. I actually started knitting this huge, chunky blanket for my mom's birthday. I got the really expensive yarn in her favorite color, this deep plum purple. I was so excited about it. I'd sit there for hours, just doing the stitches, and it was so relaxing. \n\nI also used to love cooking. I'd find these complex recipes online and spend my whole Sunday afternoon prepping meals for the week. I made this amazing roasted red pepper pasta once... and I used to read a lot, too, mostly thrillers or historical fiction, just to escape for a bit after staring at nursing textbooks all day. \n\nBut now... I don't know. It's like a switch flipped. I haven't been on that trail in months. The knitting is just sitting in a bag in the corner of my room, gathering dust. I haven't even looked at a recipe; I mostly just eat whatever is easiest, like a bowl of cereal or something, if I eat at all. And my books are just stacked on my nightstand untouched. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. I'll sit there for what feels like hours, just staring at the wall, still in my scrubs, and I just can't bring myself to care about any of the things I used to love. It all just feels so pointless now. Like, what's the point of knitting a blanket or going for a run when I'm such a mess at work? I just don't have the capacity for it anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, it's definitely been exactly like that specifically over the last two weeks, too. Probably even worse recently, honestly. I know I just went on a whole tangent about the jogging and the knitting and the cooking and the reading, but... yeah, in the past fourteen days, absolutely none of that has happened. Not even once. \n\nEvery single day recently, I walk through the door after class or a shift, and I just drop onto my bed. I don't even take my scrubs off. I just sit there. I'll look over at that knitting bag in the corner, or I'll see the books on my nightstand that I used to use to clear my head, and I just... I can't make myself care. I can't even force myself to care about finishing that blanket for my mom, even though her birthday is coming up. It's just this heavy, draining feeling where even the thought of opening a book or boiling water for pasta feels completely impossible. So yes, specifically in these last two weeks, it's been exactly what I was describing. Just me, in my scrubs, sitting on the edge of the bed for hours doing absolutely nothing and just not being able to care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really been on a scale or anything, so I can't give you an exact number, but... yeah, I think I have. I definitely have. It's not something I've been trying to do, you know? Like, I'm not on a diet or trying to count calories or anything like that. But I've noticed it mostly with my clothes. Like my work pants, the scrubs I wear to the clinic—they've been getting really loose lately. I actually had to safety-pin the waistband on my favorite pair the other day just so they wouldn't slide down while I was walking down the hall, which is just... it's embarrassing, honestly. \n\nBut it makes sense, I guess, because my appetite is just completely gone. Like I was saying about the cooking, I just don't care about food anymore. I'll wake up, go to class or go to work, and I'll just completely forget to eat. The whole day will go by, and it won't even cross my mind. And then it'll be, like, four or five in the afternoon, and I'll realize I haven't had anything but water. Even then, I don't feel hungry. My stomach might rumble a little, but the thought of actually preparing a meal or even chewing something substantial just feels exhausting. So I might force down a handful of saltine crackers just to put something in my stomach, or I'll just drink another cup of coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, but it's warm and it's easy. So yeah, I'm basically just living on coffee and crackers, and it's definitely showing. My mom even made a comment about my face looking thinner when I FaceTimed her last week, which I tried to brush off, but... yeah, the weight is definitely dropping off, even though I'm not trying to lose it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy over the past year?\n\nPatient: Elevated mood? Like... feeling really happy or having a lot of energy? No. No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt anything close to that. It's been the exact opposite for so long now. I mean, the past year has just been a constant, heavy drag. Between nursing school and clinicals, it's just been a steady decline into this... this numbness. \n\nI definitely haven't had any bursts of energy. Like I said, I barely have the energy to take my scrubs off when I get home. The idea of having *increased* energy is almost laughable right now. I don't wake up feeling refreshed or ready to take on the day, let alone feeling like I could conquer the world or something. Every morning is just a struggle to convince myself to get out from under the covers. And as for my mood, it's just flat. Or sad. Or just... empty. There haven't been any highs. No days where I suddenly felt great and wanted to do a million things. I haven't picked up extra shifts because I felt super motivated, or decided to deep clean my apartment, or suddenly wanted to go out with friends. It's just been this consistent, exhausting low. So, no, nothing like that at all. Not even for a day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a specific plan for how you would end your life?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't. I mean, I don't have a plan. I'm not sitting here figuring out how I would actually do it or, you know, looking things up or anything like that. I wouldn't actually hurt myself. But... I mean, I'd be lying if I said the thought of just... not being here hasn't crossed my mind a lot lately. \n\nIt's not about wanting to die, exactly, it's more just... I keep having these thoughts where I think about how much easier it would be if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. Like, if I could just fade out. Because everything feels so heavy, and I feel like I'm just constantly failing at everything anyway. Like with nursing school, and the clinicals, and barely eating, and letting my mom down by not finishing her present... I just keep thinking that if I could just disappear, then I wouldn't be able to mess anything else up. I wouldn't have to drag myself out of bed or force down crackers or pretend I'm okay when I'm checking a patient's vitals. It would just all stop. The pressure would stop. So, I guess I don't have a specific plan to end my life, no, but... the idea of just slipping away and not having to deal with the overwhelming exhaustion of just existing right now... that thought is there. It's there a lot, honestly. Just... wishing I could stop being a burden or a disappointment and just be gone. But no plan. No actual plan.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the overwhelming guilt, loss of interest in activities, and changes in appetite?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been a while now. I'd say about a year and two months, give or take. I remember it pretty clearly because it all started right after I graduated high school. You know, you have this idea in your head about what life is going to be like after graduation. Everyone talks about how it's this big, exciting new chapter, and you're supposed to feel so free and ready to take on the world. But for me, it was just... it was like hitting a wall. \n\nRight after graduation, I started taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic as a registered nurse, which I know is a lot for an 18-year-old to take on all at once. I thought I could handle it. I thought I was being responsible and getting a head start on my career. But almost immediately, things just started feeling wrong. The classes were harder than I expected, and the clinic was just non-stop. I remember driving home from my first week of full-time shifts and just crying in my car for twenty minutes before I could even go inside my apartment. And I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period, you know? Like, \"Oh, you're just tired, you just need to get used to the schedule, everyone feels overwhelmed at first.\" \n\nBut it didn't get better. The weeks turned into months, and instead of adjusting, I just started shutting down. The guilt started creeping in because I felt like I wasn't doing enough at work, or I wasn't studying enough for my classes, or I wasn't being a good enough daughter to my mom. And then the things I used to enjoy, like the cooking I mentioned, or even just watching a movie or talking to my friends—it all just started to feel like a chore. Like it took too much energy. And the appetite thing... that kind of snuck up on me, but looking back, I can trace it all back to that same time period, right after high school when everything just piled on. So yeah, it's been about fourteen months of just... this. Just trying to keep my head above water and feeling like I'm constantly drowning.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others cannot?\n\nPatient: Hearing voices? Like... hallucinations? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I know I've been struggling a lot, and I feel completely overwhelmed and exhausted all the time, but I haven't lost touch with reality or anything like that. I'm not seeing things that aren't there or hearing people talking to me when I'm alone. \n\nIt's actually kind of funny you ask that, because when I first started my clinicals at the clinic, we had a patient come in who was actively hallucinating, and it was so intense to witness. I remember thinking how terrifying it must be to not be able to trust your own senses. But for me, it's nothing like that. The only voice I hear is my own internal monologue, and honestly, that's bad enough right now because it's just constantly telling me how I'm failing at everything, how I'm a burden, how I can't keep up with my classes or my shifts. It's just this relentless, negative loop in my own head. But it's definitely my own thoughts, you know? Not some external voice or anything strange. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... no. The world looks exactly the same as it always has, it just feels a lot grayer and heavier. I'm just so tired. But my senses are all fine. I don't have any bizarre thoughts or beliefs, either. I know exactly who I am, where I am, and what's going on around me. I'm just... I'm just an 18-year-old girl who took on way too much, trying to balance nursing school and working as an RN, and I've just completely crashed. But no, no hallucinations or delusions or anything like that. Just the depression and the guilt and the exhaustion.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More than one episode? Um... well, it hasn't really been like... episodes, you know? It's not like it comes and goes. It's just been this one, long, continuous stretch of feeling like this. Ever since I graduated high school fourteen months ago, like I was saying, it just started and then it never stopped. 

I guess when you say "episodes," I think of someone who maybe feels terrible for a few weeks, and then they bounce back and feel totally fine and normal, and then maybe a few months later they crash again. Like a rollercoaster. But for me, there hasn't been any bouncing back. There hasn't been a break or a time where I felt like my old self again. It's just been this constant, steady weight that I've been carrying every single day since I started college and working at the clinic. 

Some days are maybe a tiny bit worse than others, like if I have a really brutal shift or if I get a bad grade on a test, the guilt and the exhaustion might feel heavier that specific day. Like last Tuesday, we were short-staffed at the clinic and I was running around for twelve hours straight, and I came home and just sat on the floor of my shower for an hour crying because I felt like I didn't do a good enough job with any of my patients. So that day was really bad. But even on the "better" days, if you can even call them that, the feeling is still there. The guilt is still there humming in the background, telling me I'm not doing enough. The exhaustion is always there. 

So, no, not multiple episodes. Just one really long, fourteen-month period of feeling completely overwhelmed and guilty and just... stuck. I keep waiting to wake up one morning and feel like it's lifted, like maybe I've finally adjusted to being an adult and balancing everything, but that morning just hasn't come yet. It's just been this one continuous downward slide since graduation.

I don't know. It's like a switch flipped. I haven't been on that trail in months. The knitting is just sitting in a bag in the corner of my room, gathering dust. I haven't even looked at a recipe; I mostly just eat whatever is easiest, like a bowl of cereal or something, if I eat at all. And my books are just stacked on my nightstand untouched. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Enjoy things? No... no, not really. It’s actually really sad when I think about it, because I used to have a whole routine, you know? Like, I used to have all these different ways to clear my head and just decompress. 

Before all this started, if I had a stressful day, I would go jogging. There’s this really nice trail right near my apartment complex, and I’d just put my headphones in and run for a few miles to burn off the stress. Or, if I was too physically tired for that, I would knit. I actually knit this huge, complicated cable-knit blanket for my mom last Christmas, and I used to spend hours looking up new patterns online. It was so relaxing. Or I’d just curl up and read a book—like, a fun fiction book, absolutely nothing to do with my college classes or nursing textbooks. And I mentioned the cooking earlier, too. I used to really love finding a new recipe, going to the store, chopping all the vegetables... it was like my special wind-down time where I could just be creative and not worry about grades or patients. 

But now... I don't know, it’s like a switch just flipped inside me. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door and I just feel... nothing. Actually, no, I feel dread, because even the thought of putting them on feels like it would take more energy than I have in my entire body. My knitting needles have just been sitting in a basket in the corner of my room gathering dust for months. I even bought some really pretty blue yarn a while back, thinking maybe it would spark something, but it's literally still sitting in the plastic shopping bag. 

Now, instead of doing any of those things after class or after a long shift at the clinic, I literally just come home, walk into my room, and sit on my bed. I don't even have the energy to change out of my work clothes half the time. I just sit there on the edge of the mattress in my scrubs, sometimes for an hour or more, just staring at the wall. I know I should get up, I know I should take a shower or make a real dinner or read a chapter of a book, but I just... I can't make myself care about any of it anymore. The jogging, the knitting, the reading, the cooking—it all just feels completely pointless. It’s so frustrating because I remember that those things used to make me happy, but when I try to force myself to do them now, it’s just blank. I just sit there in my scrubs feeling completely empty and, of course, feeling guilty that I'm wasting my evening doing absolutely nothing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely better? No. No, not at all. Like I was trying to explain a minute ago with the whole episodes thing, it just hasn't stopped. There hasn't been any extended period of time—or even a short period of time, honestly—where I felt completely free of it. I keep waiting for that to happen. I really do. 

I remember back in December, right after my first semester of nursing school ended, I had this one week off before the spring semester started. And I told myself, okay, this is it. You made it through the first semester, you survived, now you can just breathe. I had this whole plan to just sleep in and maybe watch some movies and finally feel normal again, without that constant nagging feeling that I'm failing at everything. 

But even during that week off, the guilt didn't go away. If anything, it almost got worse? Because then I was sitting at home, not studying, not at the clinic, and my brain was just screaming at me that I was being lazy. I kept thinking about my coworkers at the clinic who were probably swamped because it was flu season, and I felt so incredibly guilty for not picking up extra shifts to help them out. And I felt guilty that I wasn't using that week to get ahead on my reading for the next semester. So even when I technically had a break, and I *should* have felt better, I didn't. The feeling of being a failure just shifted from "I'm not doing enough in my classes" to "I'm wasting my time off." 

So, no, there hasn't been any extended period where it just went away. It's like... it's like wearing a heavy lead apron, you know? Like the ones you have to put on patients when you're doing x-rays? It feels like I'm wearing that all the time. From the second I wake up and look at the ceiling, to the second I finally fall asleep. I never get to take it off. I honestly don't even remember what it feels like to just exist without feeling like I'm letting everyone down, or letting myself down. It's just been this one, long, exhausting marathon for the past fourteen months, and I haven't had a single water break. I just keep running and running, and the guilt is always right there running with me.

And ever since then, it's like a loop playing in my head, over and over, showing me exactly how incompetent I am. It's like that one mistake just proves I'm not meant to be a nurse at all. I keep thinking about how much worse it could have been, and how I'm just a huge disappointment to my family, my instructors, the patients... everyone. I just start thinking about how much easier it would be if I just didn't wake up. Like, if I could just go to sleep and it would all just stop. I constantly wish I could just fade away so I wouldn't be a risk to anyone anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this overwhelming guilt and like you're a failure?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't just been since that near-miss at work, if that's what you're thinking. Yeah, about that long. It really started right after I graduated high school. Because, you know, high school was one thing, but then suddenly I was thrown into all these college classes, trying to get my prerequisites done, and at the same time I started working full-time at the clinic. It was just this massive transition. I thought I was ready for it. I really did. I had this whole plan, and I was so eager to just dive in and start helping people. \n\nBut almost immediately, it just felt like I was drowning. The classes were so much harder than I expected, and the clinic... well, the clinic is just so fast-paced and there's so much responsibility. Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. I'd come home exhausted, try to study, and just sit there staring at my textbooks, feeling like an absolute fraud. So yeah, it's been over a year of just carrying this around every single day, this constant weight telling me I'm a failure, long before that incident even happened. I mean, I used to have things that I liked doing, things that actually helped me unwind. Like, back when I first started my classes, before everything got so heavy, I used to go jogging. And if the weather was bad, I'd stay in and knit. I actually started knitting this huge, chunky blanket for my mom's birthday. I don't know. It's like a switch flipped. I haven't been on that trail in months. The knitting is just sitting in a bag in the corner of my room, gathering dust. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. I can't even force myself to care about finishing that blanket for my mom, even though her birthday is coming up. It's just this heavy, draining feeling where even the thought of opening a book or boiling water for pasta feels completely impossible. So yes, specifically in these last two weeks, it's been exactly what I was describing. Just me, in my scrubs, sitting on the edge of the bed for hours doing absolutely nothing and just not being able to care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really been on a scale or anything, so I can't give you an exact number, but... yeah, I think I have. I definitely have. It's not something I've been trying to do, you know? Like, I'm not on a diet or trying to count calories or anything like that. But I've noticed it mostly with my clothes. Like my work pants, the scrubs I wear to the clinic—they've been getting really loose lately. I actually had to safety-pin the waistband on my favorite pair the other day just so they wouldn't slide down while I was walking down the hall, which is just... it's embarrassing, honestly. \n\nBut it makes sense, I guess, because my appetite is just completely gone. Like I was saying about the cooking, I just don't care about food anymore. I'll wake up, go to class or go to work, and I'll just completely forget to eat. The whole day will go by, and it won't even cross my mind. And then it'll be, like, four or five in the afternoon, and I'll realize I haven't had anything but water. Even then, I don't feel hungry. My stomach might rumble a little, but the thought of actually preparing a meal or even chewing something substantial just feels exhausting. So I might force down a handful of saltine crackers just to put something in my stomach, or I'll just drink another cup of coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, but it's warm and it's easy. So yeah, I'm basically just living on coffee and crackers, and it's definitely showing. My mom even made a comment about my face looking thinner when I FaceTimed her last week, which I tried to brush off, but... yeah, the weight is definitely dropping off, even though I'm not trying to lose it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy over the past year?\n\nPatient: Elevated mood? Like... feeling really happy or having a lot of energy? No. No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt anything close to that. It's been the exact opposite for so long now. I mean, the past year has just been a constant, heavy drag. Between nursing school and clinicals, it's just been a steady decline into this... this numbness. \n\nI definitely haven't had any bursts of energy. Like I said, I barely have the energy to take my scrubs off when I get home. The idea of having *increased* energy is almost laughable right now. I don't wake up feeling refreshed or ready to take on the day, let alone feeling like I could conquer the world or something. Every morning is just a struggle to convince myself to get out from under the covers. And as for my mood, it's just flat. Or sad. Or just... empty. There haven't been any highs. No days where I suddenly felt great and wanted to do a million things. Or I’d just curl up and read a book—like, a fun fiction book, absolutely nothing to do with my college classes or nursing textbooks. And I mentioned the cooking earlier, too. I used to really love finding a new recipe, going to the store, chopping all the vegetables... it was like my special wind-down time where I could just be creative and not worry about grades or patients. \n\nBut now... I don't know, it’s like a switch just flipped inside me. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door and I just feel... nothing. Actually, no, I feel dread, because even the thought of putting them on feels like it would take more energy than I have in my entire body. My knitting needles have just been sitting in a basket in the corner of my room gathering dust for months. I even bought some really pretty blue yarn a while back, thinking maybe it would spark something, but it's literally still sitting in the plastic shopping bag. \n\nNow, instead of doing any of those things after class or after a long shift at the clinic, I literally just come home, walk into my room, and sit on my bed. I don't even have the energy to change out of my work clothes half the time. I just sit there on the edge of the mattress in my scrubs, sometimes for an hour or more, just staring at the wall. I know I should get up, I know I should take a shower or make a real dinner or read a chapter of a book, but I just... I can't make myself care about any of it anymore. The jogging, the knitting, the reading, the cooking—it all just feels completely pointless. It’s so frustrating because I remember that those things used to make me happy, but when I try to force myself to do them now, it’s just blank. I just sit there in my scrubs feeling completely empty and, of course, feeling guilty that I'm wasting my evening doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely better from these feelings of guilt and failure for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Completely better? No. No, not at all. Like I was trying to explain a minute ago with the whole episodes thing, it just hasn't stopped. There hasn't been any extended period of time—or even a short period of time, honestly—where I felt completely free of it. I keep waiting for that to happen. I really do. \n\nI remember back in December, right after my first semester of nursing school ended, I had this one week off before the spring semester started. And I told myself, okay, this is it. You made it through the first semester, you survived, now you can just breathe. I had this whole plan to just sleep in and maybe watch some movies and finally feel normal again, without that constant nagging feeling that I'm failing at everything. \n\nBut even during that week off, the guilt didn't go away. If anything, it almost got worse? Because then I was sitting at home, not studying, not at the clinic, and my brain was just screaming at me that I was being lazy. I kept thinking about my coworkers at the clinic who were probably swamped because it was flu season, and I felt so incredibly guilty for not picking up extra shifts to help them out. And I felt guilty that I wasn't using that week to get ahead on my reading for the next semester. So even when I technically had a break, and I *should* have felt better, I didn't. The feeling of being a failure just shifted from \"I'm not doing enough in my classes\" to \"I'm wasting my time off.\" \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any extended period where it just went away. It's like... it's like wearing a heavy lead apron, you know? Like the ones you have to put on patients when you're doing x-rays? It feels like I'm wearing that all the time. From the second I wake up and look at the ceiling, to the second I finally fall asleep. I never get to take it off. I honestly don't even remember what it feels like to just exist without feeling like I'm letting everyone down, or letting myself down. It's just been this one, long, exhausting marathon for the past fourteen months, and I haven't had a single water break. I just keep running and running, and the guilt is always right there running with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, every single day. Literally every day. I know people say every day and they mean, like, most days, or they just mean a lot of the time, but for me, I mean it literally. Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday, Sunday. It doesn't matter what day of the week it is, it doesn't matter if it's a holiday, it doesn't matter if I have the day off from the clinic. So yes, nearly every day... actually, literally every day. 

I wake up, and before I even open my eyes, before my alarm even goes off sometimes, I can feel it. It's like this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest. And I just lie there thinking about all the things I have to do, the lectures, the clinicals, the shifts, and I just feel so completely empty about all of it. And then the guilt starts, because I tell myself that I worked so hard to get into this program, I should be excited, I should be passionate, but I just... I'm not. I just drag myself out of bed, put on my scrubs, and go through the motions. 

And it's like that from the moment I wake up until the moment I finally manage to fall asleep. There are no good days anymore, you know? There are no days where I wake up and think that maybe I will go for a run today, or maybe I will pull out that blue yarn and knit today. It's just... every single day is exactly the same as the last one. Just this constant, heavy, gray feeling that never, ever takes a day off. I honestly can't think of a single twenty-four-hour period in the last fourteen months where I didn't feel this way. It's exhausting just talking about it, honestly, because when you live it day in and day out without a single break, it just completely wears you down to nothing.

    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I'm here because I just can't shake this feeling that I'm... I'm a complete failure. I mean, I'm 18, I'm just starting out as a registered nurse, taking some college classes to get ahead, and I was so excited, you know? I thought this was my calling. But lately, I just feel like I'm an absolute danger to everyone around me. There was this... this near-miss at work recently. Nobody got hurt, thank God, but it was so close. And ever since then, it's like a loop playing in my head, over and over, showing me exactly how incompetent I am. It's like that one mistake just proves I'm not meant to be a nurse at all. I keep thinking about how much worse it could have been, and how I'm just a huge disappointment to my family, my instructors, the patients... everyone. Honestly, I've been having these thoughts that they'd all just be so much better off without me getting in the way and messing everything up. It's just this heavy, awful guilt that I can't turn off, no matter how hard I try to focus on my classes or my shifts.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? I mean... no. No, I don't think so. Like, I don't have a specific plan to actually do anything to myself, if that's what you mean. Because I keep having these thoughts, and they just sort of creep in, especially when I'm driving home from a shift or when I'm lying in bed and the whole house is quiet and my brain just won't shut off. I just start thinking about how much easier it would be if I just didn't wake up. Like, if I could just go to sleep and it would all just stop. The pressure, the guilt about that near-miss at work, the constant, sickening fear that I'm going to actually hurt a patient next time... it would all just be gone. \n\nI just keep imagining what it would be like if I could just completely disappear. Not violently, just... cease to exist? Because if I wasn't here, if I was just gone, then I couldn't mess anything up ever again. I wouldn't be this huge liability on the floor, and I wouldn't be letting my instructors down in my classes. It's just this really heavy, constant feeling that my being here is the actual problem. My stomach might rumble a little, but the thought of actually preparing a meal or even chewing something substantial just feels exhausting. So I might force down a handful of saltine crackers just to put something in my stomach, or I'll just drink another cup of coffee. The coffee at the clinic is terrible, but it's warm and it's easy. So yeah, I'm basically just living on coffee and crackers, and it's definitely showing. My mom even made a comment about my face looking thinner when I FaceTimed her last week, which I tried to brush off, but... yeah, the weight is definitely dropping off, even though I'm not trying to lose it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy over the past year?\n\nPatient: Elevated mood? Like... feeling really happy or having a lot of energy? No. No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt anything close to that. It's been the exact opposite for so long now. I mean, the past year has just been a constant, heavy drag. Between nursing school and clinicals, it's just been a steady decline into this... this numbness. \n\nI definitely haven't had any bursts of energy. Like I said, I barely have the energy to take my scrubs off when I get home. The idea of having *increased* energy is almost laughable right now. I don't wake up feeling refreshed or ready to take on the day, let alone feeling like I could conquer the world or something. Every morning is just a struggle to convince myself to get out from under the covers. And as for my mood, it's just flat. Or sad. Or just... empty. There haven't been any highs. No days where I suddenly felt great and wanted to do a million things. I haven't picked up extra shifts because I felt super motivated, or decided to deep clean my apartment, or suddenly wanted to go out with friends. It's just been this consistent, exhausting low. So, no, nothing like that at all. Not even for a day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a specific plan for how you would end your life?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't. I mean, I don't have a plan. I'm not sitting here figuring out how I would actually do it or, you know, looking things up or anything like that. I wouldn't actually hurt myself. But... I mean, I'd be lying if I said the thought of just... not being here hasn't crossed my mind a lot lately. \n\nIt's not about wanting to die, exactly, it's more just... I keep having these thoughts where I think about how much easier it would be if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. Like, if I could just fade out. Because everything feels so heavy, and I feel like I'm just constantly failing at everything anyway. Like with nursing school, and the clinicals, and barely eating, and letting my mom down by not finishing her present... I just keep thinking that if I could just disappear, then I wouldn't be able to mess anything else up. I wouldn't have to drag myself out of bed or force down crackers or pretend I'm okay when I'm checking a patient's vitals. It would just all stop. The pressure would stop. So, I guess I don't have a specific plan to end my life, no, but... the idea of just slipping away and not having to deal with the overwhelming exhaustion of just existing right now... that thought is there. It's there a lot, honestly. Just... wishing I could stop being a burden or a disappointment and just be gone. But no plan. No actual plan.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the overwhelming guilt, loss of interest in activities, and changes in appetite?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been a while now. I'd say about a year and two months, give or take. I remember it pretty clearly because it all started right after I graduated high school. You know, you have this idea in your head about what life is going to be like after graduation. Everyone talks about how it's this big, exciting new chapter, and you're supposed to feel so free and ready to take on the world. But for me, it was just... it was like hitting a wall. \n\nRight after graduation, I started taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic as a registered nurse, which I know is a lot for an 18-year-old to take on all at once. I thought I could handle it. I thought I was being responsible and getting a head start on my career. But almost immediately, things just started feeling wrong. The classes were harder than I expected, and the clinic was just non-stop. I remember driving home from my first week of full-time shifts and just crying in my car for twenty minutes before I could even go inside my apartment. And I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period, you know? Like, \"Oh, you're just tired, you just need to get used to the schedule, everyone feels overwhelmed at first.\" \n\nBut it didn't get better. The weeks turned into months, and instead of adjusting, I just started shutting down. The guilt started creeping in because I felt like I wasn't doing enough at work, or I wasn't studying enough for my classes, or I wasn't being a good enough daughter to my mom. And then the things I used to enjoy, like the cooking I mentioned, or even just watching a movie or talking to my friends—it all just started to feel like a chore. Like it took too much energy. And the appetite thing... that kind of snuck up on me, but looking back, I can trace it all back to that same time period, right after high school when everything just piled on. So yeah, it's been about fourteen months of just... this. Just trying to keep my head above water and feeling like I'm constantly drowning.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others cannot?\n\nPatient: Hearing voices? Like... hallucinations? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I know I've been struggling a lot, and I feel completely overwhelmed and exhausted all the time, but I haven't lost touch with reality or anything like that. I'm not seeing things that aren't there or hearing people talking to me when I'm alone. \n\nIt's actually kind of funny you ask that, because when I first started my clinicals at the clinic, we had a patient come in who was actively hallucinating, and it was so intense to witness. I remember thinking how terrifying it must be to not be able to trust your own senses. But for me, it's nothing like that. The only voice I hear is my own internal monologue, and honestly, that's bad enough right now because it's just constantly telling me how I'm failing at everything, how I'm a burden, how I can't keep up with my classes or my shifts. It's just this relentless, negative loop in my own head. But it's definitely my own thoughts, you know? Not some external voice or anything strange. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... no. The world looks exactly the same as it always has, it just feels a lot grayer and heavier. I'm just so tired. But my senses are all fine. I don't have any bizarre thoughts or beliefs, either. I know exactly who I am, where I am, and what's going on around me. I'm just... I'm just an 18-year-old girl who took on way too much, trying to balance nursing school and working as an RN, and I've just completely crashed. But no, no hallucinations or delusions or anything like that. Just the depression and the guilt and the exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this overwhelmed and guilty in the past year?\n\nPatient: More than one episode? Um... well, it hasn't really been like... episodes, you know? It's not like it comes and goes. It's just been this one, long, continuous stretch of feeling like this. Ever since I graduated high school fourteen months ago, like I was saying, it just started and then it never stopped. \n\nI guess when you say \"episodes,\" I think of someone who maybe feels terrible for a few weeks, and then they bounce back and feel totally fine and normal, and then maybe a few months later they crash again. Like a rollercoaster. But for me, there hasn't been any bouncing back. There hasn't been a break or a time where I felt like my old self again. It's just been this constant, steady weight that I've been carrying every single day since I started college and working at the clinic. \n\nSome days are maybe a tiny bit worse than others, like if I have a really brutal shift or if I get a bad grade on a test, the guilt and the exhaustion might feel heavier that specific day. Like last Tuesday, we were short-staffed at the clinic and I was running around for twelve hours straight, and I came home and just sat on the floor of my shower for an hour crying because I felt like I didn't do a good enough job with any of my patients. So that day was really bad. But even on the \"better\" days, if you can even call them that, the feeling is still there. The guilt is still there humming in the background, telling me I'm not doing enough. The exhaustion is always there. \n\nSo, no, not multiple episodes. Just one really long, fourteen-month period of feeling completely overwhelmed and guilty and just... stuck. I keep waiting to wake up one morning and feel like it's lifted, like maybe I've finally adjusted to being an adult and balancing everything, but that morning just hasn't come yet. It's just been this one continuous downward slide since graduation.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Enjoy things? No... no, not really. It’s actually really sad when I think about it, because I used to have a whole routine, you know? Like, I used to have all these different ways to clear my head and just decompress. \n\nBefore all this started, if I had a stressful day, I would go jogging. There’s this really nice trail right near my apartment complex, and I’d just put my headphones in and run for a few miles to burn off the stress. Or, if I was too physically tired for that, I would knit. I actually knit this huge, complicated cable-knit blanket for my mom last Christmas, and I used to spend hours looking up new patterns online. It was so relaxing. Or I’d just curl up and read a book—like, a fun fiction book, absolutely nothing to do with my college classes or nursing textbooks. And I mentioned the cooking earlier, too. I used to really love finding a new recipe, going to the store, chopping all the vegetables... it was like my special wind-down time where I could just be creative and not worry about grades or patients. \n\nBut now... I don't know, it’s like a switch just flipped inside me. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door and I just feel... nothing. Actually, no, I feel dread, because even the thought of putting them on feels like it would take more energy than I have in my entire body. My knitting needles have just been sitting in a basket in the corner of my room gathering dust for months. I even bought some really pretty blue yarn a while back, thinking maybe it would spark something, but it's literally still sitting in the plastic shopping bag. \n\nNow, instead of doing any of those things after class or after a long shift at the clinic, I literally just come home, walk into my room, and sit on my bed. I don't even have the energy to change out of my work clothes half the time. I just sit there on the edge of the mattress in my scrubs, sometimes for an hour or more, just staring at the wall. I know I should get up, I know I should take a shower or make a real dinner or read a chapter of a book, but I just... I can't make myself care about any of it anymore. The jogging, the knitting, the reading, the cooking—it all just feels completely pointless. It’s so frustrating because I remember that those things used to make me happy, but when I try to force myself to do them now, it’s just blank. I just sit there in my scrubs feeling completely empty and, of course, feeling guilty that I'm wasting my evening doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely better from these feelings of guilt and failure for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Completely better? No. No, not at all. Like I was trying to explain a minute ago with the whole episodes thing, it just hasn't stopped. There hasn't been any extended period of time—or even a short period of time, honestly—where I felt completely free of it. I keep waiting for that to happen. I really do. \n\nI remember back in December, right after my first semester of nursing school ended, I had this one week off before the spring semester started. And I told myself, okay, this is it. You made it through the first semester, you survived, now you can just breathe. I had this whole plan to just sleep in and maybe watch some movies and finally feel normal again, without that constant nagging feeling that I'm failing at everything. \n\nBut even during that week off, the guilt didn't go away. If anything, it almost got worse? Because then I was sitting at home, not studying, not at the clinic, and my brain was just screaming at me that I was being lazy. I kept thinking about my coworkers at the clinic who were probably swamped because it was flu season, and I felt so incredibly guilty for not picking up extra shifts to help them out. And I felt guilty that I wasn't using that week to get ahead on my reading for the next semester. So even when I technically had a break, and I *should* have felt better, I didn't. The feeling of being a failure just shifted from \"I'm not doing enough in my classes\" to \"I'm wasting my time off.\" \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any extended period where it just went away. It's like... it's like wearing a heavy lead apron, you know? Like the ones you have to put on patients when you're doing x-rays? It feels like I'm wearing that all the time. From the second I wake up and look at the ceiling, to the second I finally fall asleep. I never get to take it off. I honestly don't even remember what it feels like to just exist without feeling like I'm letting everyone down, or letting myself down. It's just been this one, long, exhausting marathon for the past fourteen months, and I haven't had a single water break. I just keep running and running, and the guilt is always right there running with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with the overwhelming guilt and loss of interest, nearly every day since it started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, every single day. Literally every day. I know people say every day and they mean, like, most days, or they just mean a lot of the time, but for me, I mean it literally. Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday, Sunday. It doesn't matter what day of the week it is, it doesn't matter if it's a holiday, it doesn't matter if I have the day off from the clinic. So yes, nearly every day... actually, literally every day. \n\nI wake up, and before I even open my eyes, before my alarm even goes off sometimes, I can feel it. It's like this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest. And I just lie there thinking about all the things I have to do, the lectures, the clinicals, the shifts, and I just feel so completely empty about all of it. And then the guilt starts, because I tell myself that I worked so hard to get into this program, I should be excited, I should be passionate, but I just... I'm not. I just drag myself out of bed, put on my scrubs, and go through the motions. \n\nAnd it's like that from the moment I wake up until the moment I finally manage to fall asleep. There are no good days anymore, you know? There are no days where I wake up and think that maybe I will go for a run today, or maybe I will pull out that blue yarn and knit today. It's just... every single day is exactly the same as the last one. Just this constant, heavy, gray feeling that never, ever takes a day off. I honestly can't think of a single twenty-four-hour period in the last fourteen months where I didn't feel this way. It's exhausting just talking about it, honestly, because when you live it day in and day out without a single break, it just completely wears you down to nothing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Like I think I just mentioned a second ago, it's been fourteen months. So, about a year and two months of this. 

It all really started right after I graduated high school, which is... it's just really depressing to think about, honestly. Because you know how high school graduation is supposed to be this huge, exciting milestone? I thought I could just power through it all. I told myself that I could handle college and a full-time job, that I just needed to be disciplined. But the reality of trying to balance the heavy coursework with being at the clinic full-time... it just completely drained whatever was left inside of me. 

So ever since that transition, for a solid year and two months now, I've been stuck in this exact same spot. And fourteen months might not sound like a whole lifetime to some people, but when you're dragging yourself through every single day without a single break from the guilt or that heavy, empty feeling I was talking about, it really does feel like an eternity. I keep looking back at my graduation pictures, trying to remember what it felt like to actually be excited about my future, because ever since those college classes and the full-time clinic work actually started, there hasn't been any significant improvement at all. Just a year and two months of waking up and feeling like I'm already failing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two weeks? Oh, definitely. I mean, way, way more than two weeks. Like I was saying about the fourteen months, it's just a constant, everyday thing. Most days, I wake up with this... this really heavy, empty feeling right in the center of my chest. It’s hard to even describe because it’s like a void, but somehow it weighs a ton? 

And the hardest part is that I still have to get up and function. I drive to the clinic, and I spend the entire day putting on this huge performance, trying to act completely normal. I'll be in the exam room, smiling at our patients, speaking to them in Spanish, trying to be this warm, reassuring presence for them. A lot of our Spanish-speaking patients get really nervous about coming in, so I always try to be super upbeat and comforting to put them at ease. I'll be standing there, smiling, going through the motions of taking their blood pressure and chatting with them, but inside... inside, I feel like I'm just barely holding myself together. 

It takes every single ounce of energy I have just to keep that smile on my face and keep my voice steady. It’s like I’m a glass of water filled right to the very brim, and I have to walk across a crowded room all day without spilling a single drop. It's so incredibly draining to feel that empty and heavy on the inside while having to project all this fake sunshine on the outside. So, yes, it's been far more than two weeks of feeling this way most of the time. It's just my reality right now, just trying to survive my shifts without anyone at the clinic noticing how close I am to just completely falling apart.

Every day I'd go in and just feel this overwhelming sense that I didn't belong there, that everyone else knew exactly what they were doing and I was just faking it, waiting to be found out. I mean, I used to have things that I liked doing, things that actually helped me unwind. Like, back when I first started my classes, before everything got so heavy, I used to go jogging. And if the weather was bad, I'd stay in and knit. I actually started knitting this huge, chunky blanket for my mom's birthday. I got the really expensive yarn in her favorite color, this deep plum purple. I was so excited about it. I'd sit there for hours, just doing the stitches, and it was so relaxing. \n\nI also used to love cooking. I'd find these complex recipes online and spend my whole Sunday afternoon prepping meals for the week. I don't know. It's like a switch flipped. I haven't been on that trail in months. The knitting is just sitting in a bag in the corner of my room, gathering dust. I haven't even looked at a recipe; I mostly just eat whatever is easiest, like a bowl of cereal or something, if I eat at all. And my books are just stacked on my nightstand untouched. Now, when I get home from a shift or from class, I don't even have the energy to change out of my scrubs. I just... I just go to my room and sit on the edge of my bed. I'll sit there for what feels like hours, just staring at the wall, still in my scrubs, and I just can't bring myself to care about any of the things I used to love. Like my work pants, the scrubs I wear to the clinic—they've been getting really loose lately. Like I was saying about the cooking, I just don't care about food anymore. I'll wake up, go to class or go to work, and I'll just completely forget to eat.
